- [Qualifier](/wiki/qualifier)

In Al-Anon circles, "Qualifier" or "Q" refers to a person in someone's life whose drinking or substance use patterns have led them to ("qualified" them for) attendance at an Al-Anon meeting.

- [Al Anon](/wiki/al-anon)

Al-Anon is an anonymous support group for those who have been impacted by someone else's drinking and substance use patterns. Although the term isn't originally from the program's literature, "Q" for "Qualifier" has been used to describe a person whose drinking patterns are an ongoing factor in someone's life. https://al-anon.org/

- [Hypoarousal](/wiki/hypoarousal)

This is in contrast to hyperarousal, though notably, a stressed nervous system may swing abruptly from one to the other. Signs of hypoarousal include: Emotional numbness Low energy or chronic fatigue Brain fog or trouble concentrating Dissociation

- [Opioid](/wiki/opioid)

Opioids are a class of drugs that bind to opioid receptors in the brain and body, which can block pain signals and cause feelings of euphoria. Common examples include codeine, fentanyl, and heroin. Chronic use increases the risk of higher tolerance, dependence, and Opioid Use Disorder (OUD). Harm-reduction frameworks frequently include Naloxone (Narcan) distribution to reverse opioid overdoses.

- [Opioid Crisis](/wiki/opioid-crisis)

The "opioid crisis" in the United States refers to a decades-long epidemic of opioid use and overdoses. About 80,000 people died from overdoses involving opioids in 2023, though fatalities peaked in 2022 and have dropped about 12% year-over-year. Opioid Use Disorder (OUD) is a more specific category of Substance Use Disorder (SUD), and there are dedicated Opioid Treatment Programs (OTPs) that are licensed to provide therapy, medication, and peer support. Historically, the term morphinism described essentially the same diagnosis.

- [Opium](/wiki/opium)

Opium is a natural narcotic made from the seeds of the poppy plant, containing morphine, codeine, and thebaine as active alkaloids. It's the primary source from which other opiates are derived, including heroin. Some of the earliest records of the poppy plant being cultivated date back to ancient Mesopotamia around 3400 BCE.

- [Pharmacodynamics](/wiki/pharmacodynamics)

Pharmacodynamics (PD) is the study of a drug's effects on the body. Most drugs act on cellular receptors that activate (agonist ) or block (antagonist) them to produce a desired reaction.

- [Pharmacogenetics](/wiki/pharmacogenetics)

Pharmacogenetics is the study of how a person's genes influence the way a drug is metabolized. It's considered a more narrow term than pharmacogenomics, but both are often used interchangeably. For any particular substance consumed, someone might be considered a poor metabolizer, an intermediate metabolizer, or an ultra-rapid metabolizer. Poor metabolizers might break down a drug too slowly and…

- [Pharmacogenomics](/wiki/pharmacogenomics)

Pharmacogenomics is the study of how one's total genetic profile impacts the interactions a drug has in their body. The term is used almost interchangeably with pharmacogenetics, which has a slightly more narrow definition.

- [Pharmacokinetics](/wiki/pharmacokinetics)

Pharmacokinetics describes the changes a body makes to a drug after consumption, as the drug goes through absorption, distribution, metabolism, and elimination (ADME). It's a central topic in psychopharmacology.

Phencyclidine, also called angel dust or PCP, is an addictive drug that causes dissociation, euphoria, mood swings, hallucinations, and paranoia. - [Pope Urban Vii](/wiki/pope-urban-vii)

In 1590, Pope Urban VII issued perhaps the first ban on smoking, threatening anyone with excommunication for using tobacco in or near a church. It covered smoking, chewing, or sniffing snuff. He died of malaria and holds the record for the shortest papacy in history: 12 days.

- [Pope Urban Viii](/wiki/pope-urban-viii)

Unlike his predecessor who held the papacy for 12 days, Pope Urban VIII reigned for 21 years. He prohibited the use of tobacco in holy places under the threat of excommunication in a 1624 decree. Supposedly, the excessive sneezing and grime from chewing tobacco felt disruptive and disrespectful.

Rehab (rehabilitation) refers to a program or facility that restores something or someone to an improved condition. In the context of treatment for substance use disorders, rehab can refer to a level of care between medical detoxification, residential treatment centers, inpatient facilities, partial hospitalization programs, and intensive outpatient programs.

- [Rehab Riviera](/wiki/rehab-riviera)

The term "Rehab Riviera" is a term to describe the massive concentration of rehabilitation centers and recovery resources in Southern California, especially around Orange County. It often has a negative implication due to widely-shared reporting about predatory business models and regulatory gaps in the network of private rehabilitation centers.

- [Relapse](/wiki/relapse)

A relapse is the return of a condition after a period of its absence or remission. In the context of substance use, it normally means using substances actively again after a period of sobriety or reduction in use. In recovery circles, relapse is often considered a gradual process that involves emotional, mental, and physical dimensions. Some failure in routine or external stressor might trigger…

- [Relapse Prevention](/wiki/relapse-prevention)

Relapse prevention refers to coping skills developed to manage the triggers, risk factors, or warnings signs of a relapse. The Marlatt and Gordon Relapse Prevention Model is a cognitive-behavioral framework developed by the same researchers who noted the Abstinence Violation Effect (AVE). The CENAPS Model proposes nine principles to sustain recovery: Stabilization Assessment Relapse Education…

- [Release Of Information Roi](/wiki/release-of-information-roi)

A Release of Information (ROI) is a document that allows a healthcare provider to share Protected Health Information (PHI) with another entity. - [Remission](/wiki/remission)

Remission describes a state when symptoms of a disease decrease or disappear. It's a narrower concept than recovery, which describes the entire process of healing towards an improved quality of life. In the DSM-5-TR, remission for substance use disorder (SUD) means that someone who used to meet its diagnostic criteria no longer meets any except cravings. Early remission is considered the period…

- [Screen Affect](/wiki/screen-affect)

In psychology, a screen affect is a defense mechanism where a more manageable emotion is substituted for a deeper one that becomes repressed. For instance, someone might romanticize the past as a screen affect to block out an early trauma, or someone might act angry to mask the fear of being abandoned.

- [Scrip Doctors](/wiki/scrip-doctors)

"Scrip doctors" is a term for doctors who illegally or irresponsibly write large numbers of prescriptions, which often end up being resold for recreational use. They might work out of a facility or center called a "pill mill," and the most common substances acquired through them include opioids like hydrocodone, muscle relaxants like Soma, and benzodiazepines like Xanax.

Vivitrol is a prescription medication that contains naltrexone and is used to treat Alcohol Use Disorder (AUD) and Opioid Use Disorder (OUD). It's administered as an intramuscular injection once a month to block the receptors in the brain that produce the rewarding effects of alcohol and opioids.

- [Zyklon B](/wiki/zyklon-b)

Zyklon B was the name for a cyanide-based pesticide developed in 1920s Germany. It was also one of the main chemicals used by the Nazis in gas chambers during the Holocaust for mass extermination. After WWII ended, executives and directors of the companies that supplied Zyklon B were tried and executed for war crimes.
